New data, which lead us to: First, to make a new insurance reform, including our political intentions and the development of our own political positions, which we have tabled in an election and which will now be done, with a new insurance policy that does not penalise productivity, creativity, with a new insurance policy, which will reduce insurance contributions throughout the whole economy, with a new insurance system that will reduce insurance contributions to the tax winge, the so-called 'insurance wedge' of workers, so as to increase employment, with a new insurance policy that will eliminate the complexity and bureaucracy of the pensioner, with a new insurance system that will create new restructurings by creating economies of scale, with a new insurance policy that will be in the service of the citizen, the worker, the pensioner and above all in a new insurance system, which will once again return to the public confidence of the Greeks, and will return to all the Greeks. We want all Greeks to retrust our insurance system, to trust it, to come, to pay contributions, because in the end they will receive a decent and adequate pension. On this basis, therefore, you know that we have already said that on the basis of alignment of the decisions of the Council of State, once the insurance law has been passed, pensions that have been cut, Mr and Mr, over EUR 1.300, as was the Katrugalos Act, have to be increased. Secondly, we will change the replacement coefficients so that the system will again be recompense, that it will become a system that we really deserve to have, a system that is attractive, that grows insurance consciousness. That is why we are looking through the National Analogue Authority, which is currently trying to see through studies the fiscal potential and the impact it will have, so that we can apply this reciprocity to the system. At the same time, all these changes, which I said before, come to be combined with the other change, which was also considered unconstitutional, the existing framework for a million four hundred and forty thousand more free professionals, self-employed and farmers, who have been dealing with the way they have so far paid contributions, namely by linking income to their insurance payments and debts. The Ministry of Defence shall select real estate for use by the National Defence Fund, the so-called Hellenic Army Equity Fund, the National Fleet Fund, the Navy Equity Fund, the Air Defence Fund and the Equity Fund, and shall launch electronic auctions for their rental. In order to release any real estate in the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in order to open a competitive procedure for the use of this property, there is, of course, a decision of the shareholder funds bodies. It's not a simple process, the Minister says it with the congressman, the mayor comes, he makes a suggestion, and go, let's get rid of it. It's not a cow for milking the property of the Armed Forces in general. That is what I want us to understand that there are specific procedures. The management of the EIB's equity funds is accounted for by the members of the general assembly, i.e. the members of the Armed Forces. The assets of the share funds are available if they are deemed to be inactive and can therefore enter the list of real estate for use. The exploitation may include a privatisation on the part of an investment proposal, an investment project, and it would be appropriate to come up with such investment projects - they will confer benefits on the Armed Forces - or they could well include a concession to a local government body for housing renewal. But as long as the asset is active, it's out of the candidacy, if you like, to be used by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. This is how this process works, and I think it is a process of reasonable, transparent and a process that must - because this is the final analysis - to benefit the Armed Forces as well. Therefore, since Camp Koskina is active at the moment, there is, it works for some military purposes for which I cannot say anything more specific, because this is a matter of military intelligence, it acts as a train station, a military vehicle maintenance plant, a repair or anything else, at this time it is not available for use. In order to be made available for use, it must be made inactive so that the competent fund to which it belongs is able to address the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and say "to, we have this property, what we can do, look at it either by means of a tender for renting, selling, exploiting or programming with a municipality for its use". That's the process. Whatever we do, whatever intentions we have - I do not question good intentions - we cannot escape it, because this is the process of exploiting the assets of the Armed Forces.

The Conference of Presidents decided at its meeting on 26 November 2019 to discuss the draft law at a single meeting on the principle of articles and amendments. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u247	The House therefore agreed. The rapporteur of the New Democracy, Mr Dimitrios Kouvelas, has the floor. Please open the system of electronic recordings. Here you go, Mr. Kouvela.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u248	Thank you very much, Mr President. Mr Ministers, ladies and gentlemen, we are debating in plenary following an in-depth debate and an exchange of worthwhile views and putting forward constructive proposals to the committee, a draft law that really contributes to the effective stimulus to the institution of justice, because all these years of the unprecedented crisis which has hit our country, unfortunately, top institutions have been deceived in the eyes of the citizens. These institutions must be protected and restored. Justice is one of the main pillars of our democracy. In any case, it is a modern legislative framework which will help to bring about a more rapid and effective administration of justice, which is a precondition for the economic and social development of our country, a legislative framework that is fully compatible with the rule of law which is the refuge of the weak, above all. This project actually comes to contribute to something very important. On the one hand, the quickest solution to private disputes at a lower cost to the benefit of citizens, on the other hand, to reduce the legal material, through an alternative institution, of mediation. Now, what differences fall within the institution with its gradual start-up. First of all, there are all the differences which fall under the regular procedure of the Multilateral Court of First Instance and relate to actions to be filed as from 15 January 2020. It is the family differences, other than the matrimonial differences, which relate to actions to be filed as from 15 March 2020 and finally, the differences, which are subject to the regular procedure of the one-sided Court of First Instance, concerning the actions to be filed from 15 May 2020 onwards. The aim of this draft law is to ensure that the institution of mediation operates as a credible additional method of resolving private disputes before resorting to the justice procedure before, in other words, bringing an action according to the above. Therefore, we do not deprive anyone of his natural judge. We do, Minister, have a draft law which is a sign of good legislature and is coming to amend, improve, heal, failures and omissions of the two previous relevant laws, of PASOK government Law No.3898/2010 and of SYRIZA - ANEL government No. 4512/2018. It is because some colleagues from these parties express themselves positively for this draft law, but they complain that we are simply copying them, that this is not a simple copy paste. It's clear, it's clear. I would remind you that the first law simply did not walk in practice, nor was it even backed up by the necessary information to the citizens, as the Minister himself recommended at that time, not on his own responsibility, however. And the other recent law of the SYRIZA government simply did not work, as its provisions were tripled from a constitutional point of view in the administrative plenary session of the Ice Bay. I am making this brief historical reference to see where we are today and what needs we need to address. What we are doing with this legislative intervention is to align ourselves with the judgment of the Ice Bay, as well as with the judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union, which had judged as particularly restrictive the criteria laid down by Greek legislation for the process of setting up mediators. In addition, in the process of drawing up this draft law, account has been taken of the European and international good practice of this institution, and this practice and its effects have been reflected in this legislation. Of course the Ministry's leadership showed its good intentions, listening carefully and taking into account good ideas and suggestions from wherever they came. As a lawyer, but also all colleagues in the Chamber, we have experienced the complaints, the concerns of our citizens and our clients, and our own personal concerns about the delay in the administration of justice, but also about its quality, despite the efforts made in recent years to improve the functioning of our judicial system. The consequence of this is to feel insecure as regards the administration of justice, which often goes to the limits of mistrial and veto, mainly because of the excessive number of cases the judges are called upon to judge. And to avoid misunderstandings, the institution of mediation is a tool of extrajudicial resolution of private differences in less time and less cost. It does not replace justice, it does not annul it, it does not supplement it, but it improves it by reducing the burden of the courts. The Ombudsman is not a judge, nor an arbitrator, nor a judge of the dispute. Its task is to make it easier for the parties to come to a consensual settlement of their dispute. This is the ambition and the result required in practice by the institution that was essentially introduced into the European legal order by Directive 52/2008 of the European Parliament and of the Council, while functionally, it is actually introduced into the Greek legal order by this draft law. As regards Articles, from the first to the ninth, they are mainly legal provisions, whereas from Articles 10 to 33, they are more technical matters relating to mediation and the procedure. I distinguish between the following elements and points which indicate the value of this legislation. Point one: a separate law on mediation is now in place. It is very important for all those involved in an institution or a field of law to know from the outset that only one piece of legislation is sufficient and can resort to it. This is important, ladies and gentlemen, as the totality of provisions makes it very difficult for judges, lawyers and, in general, for the officials of justice. Point 2: This legislation is based on European and international experience, with the best example of Italy, which has led to a large number of successful mediation. So he unloaded the courts and contributed to a faster and more efficient, but also a more economic solution to a significant number of private individuals. Point 3: Mandatory recourse to mediation is currently limited solely to an initial session of EUR 50 for the Ombudsman, and from now on the process will only continue if the parties so wish, without even having to reach an agreement. A solution is also foreseen in the event that the parties do not find it in the person of the Ombudsman, this initial compulsory session. It will then be the Central Intermediary Committee which will be appointed by a miner from the local appropriate register. However, disputes in which a party is a public, local and legal party are excluded from the compulsory first meeting are generally excluded from the provisions of this draft law. Point 4: The compulsory presence of a legal document, i.e. a lawyer for each of the parties - except for cases of minor disputes and consumer disputes - gives increased authority to the institution, but also security in the procedure, since citizens are unable to know legal aspects of their case, which are raised in mediation. In addition, the lawyer guarantees the protection of interests, in particular the weakest parties. We realise how important the role of the lawyer, who is guaranteed by this legislation in the mediation process, is. It is important not only in the stages of a trial, but also here that we are actually talking about an alternative form of dispute resolution, out-of-court proceedings. Point 5: This piece of legislation cures unconstitutional failures in relation to the cost of the procedure and other points, since the costs have now fallen quite well for the benefit of citizens, according to the European cheques, who do not want a great deal of extra burden for the citizen, when he wants to try to resolve his dispute, either alternatively or through recourse to justice. The remuneration of both lawyers and the ombudsman shared by both parties shall be determined by agreement of the parties concerned. Point 6: The mediation practice drawn up at the end of the procedure and its filing by either party in the local court’s competent secretariat, accompanied by the payment of the paradox only EUR 50, is a strong enforceable title, in accordance with Article 904 of the Code of Civil Procedure. Point 7: Includes the suspension of limitations, amortisation deadlines and the procedural deadlines of the Code of Civil Procedure and the legal effects obtained by holding the mandatory first session, but also the full - if any - of voluntary mediation. All of this is regulated in detail, without any doubt as to exactly which regulation is in force in any case. Eighth point: Legislation incorporates principles and rules of ethics, which must govern the functioning of the institution and the behaviour of the Ombudsman, with a further proportional application of the European Code of Conduct, where required. On that basis, the behaviour of the ombudsmen shall be monitored and evaluated and disciplinary penalties provided for as appropriate. So far as the institution is concerned, the principle of impartiality, independence, neutrality and, of course, the confidentiality of the Ombudsman in the performance of his duties is concerned. Point Ninth: This Legislation lays down in detail the composition, operation and responsibilities of the Central Mediation Committee and the powers of four subcommittees operating within it. The conditions for authorisation and the way in which the training providers of candidates for ombudsmen can be either legal persons under private law involving legal associations, training centres and lifelong learning centres of our higher education institutions, or natural or legal persons legally established and intended to provide such training. Finally, provision is made for the minimum content of the educational programme, the matter being examined and the examination procedure for the accreditation of ombudsmen. There is therefore no doubt at all how these issues will develop in the light of comments, suggestions, ideas from all sides. Point 10th: This legislation establishes systematic monitoring of the functions and results of the mediation institution by means of reports submitted by ombudsmen to the Central Mediation Committee, but also from the last to the Ministry of Justice, with a view to continuous improvement and promotion of the institution. Point 11: This is an issue which has been raised again by all the wings of the Greek Parliament, as well as by those of those who are interested in the institution. It is that of legal aid to low-income citizens who find it difficult to respond even to this low-cost process, the compulsory first session, but then. This makes it easier for all citizens to access mediation. Indeed, this draft law gives all of us the opportunity to realise that the government, the political leadership of the Ministry of Justice and all of us are aiming to transform, over time, the mediation process into a conscious choice among the citizens, with direct benefits in two directions, both towards reducing the judiciary and decommissioning the functioning of justice and, more generally, for the benefit of citizens at cost and mainly of time. Obviously in Greece the culture of recourse to the institution is not developed and it is necessary to cultivate it. No law can do that. It requires that everyone involved or interested in establishing the institution of mediation, with the edge of the Ministry of Justice, should be involved in an information campaign with citizens and not just citizens. In conclusion, I will repeat the proposal that I made in committee, Minister, to familiarise myself with the institution of mediation already since secondary education and to introduce mediation as a special lesson in legal schools throughout the country. Only in this way will the culture we want be cultivated so that the institution can operate in the long term and effectively in practice. At the same time, a ministerial amendment, which regulates a number of urgent issues - the Minister will refer to it - and the administrative autonomy of the very important 'Home of the Child' structure, i.e. the office for the protection of minors. Here I invite you all to vote for the bill and the ministerial amendment as a whole. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u249	The general rapporteur for SYRIZA Mr Dionysios - Charalambos Kalamatianos has the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u250	Thank you, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, 'intermediation' means the structured process in which two or more parties voluntarily attempt to resolve an agreement with the Ombudsman. Intermediation is not a joint negotiation or compromise. It has rules and a specific procedure to be followed. The rules shall be adapted to the requirements of the Parties. The five-fold mediation is the introduction of a third, neutral and independent person, a trained mediator, which will help the disputing parties in the negotiation process to find a commonly accepted solution. In other words, the dispute is resolved by agreement between the parties, which have full control of this agreement. Law 3898/2010 introduced incomplete and with no effect the institution of mediation in our country. The institution, with the responsibility of the governments of New Democracy and PASOK, has not worked in our country and has not been able to embrace society. This law has essentially allowed the creation of a mediation training industry. In 2014, the government committed itself to creditors that mediation would be re-regulated by provisions, which would be part of a memorandum law. This explains why the institution of mediation and the law-making of SYRIZA was integrated into a law, Law 4512. For the above reasons, the SYRIZA government, after taking over the country's governance in 2015, took measures to strengthen the institution in a related context. There was cooperation with the European Commission. A special legislative committee was set up for a year and a half, with the cooperation of judges, university professors and lawyers. The Legislative Committee assessed the institution and tabled the legislative proposals, which became state law with Law 4512/2018. Under this law, Articles 178 to 206, the mediation process was reorganised on a proper basis. Innovations, such as the functioning of the Central Mediation Committee, were introduced with the participation of five judges, so that there would also be a similar institutionality. The doubling of the training hours of the ombudsmen was foreseen by this law, as well as the reduction in the costs of training. State universities were also introduced into the training of ombudsmen. Certifications have been modernised. Obligation has been laid down in certain differences and many other positive provisions. I'm going to be a little more focused on the very important innovation that was introduced by v. 4512, i.e. the need for lawyers to be present at the mandatory initial session. This will strengthen legal certainty, especially for the weaker part. It is ensured that there will be no possibility of one party appearing with a legal representation and the other without, thereby undermining the equality of arms, impartiality and equal distance. The draft that came for consultation was a possible performance of lawyers and it is positive that the Ministry should be changed as to the compulsoryity of their performance. The draft law, which is introduced, deals with and copies the legislative initiative of SYRIZA. It effectively abolishes the provisions of Law 4512 and restores them to specific points. There is therefore a alleged introduction or reform of the institution, but it is not in practice. No innovative or rhetoric contributes to the country's legal order. The question therefore arises why you have not confined yourself to amendments to the articles of Law 4512, but you have gone on to abolish them globally. The General Accounting Office of the State, on page 71 of its report on this draft law, states: ‘In particular, the provisions of existing legislation, Articles 178 to 206, of Law 4512 shall be repeated’. The General Accounting Office, ladies and gentlemen, says that exactly. The issues raised by the administrative plenary of the Ice Bay were focused on the question of the remuneration of lawyers to the compulsory performance. This was obviously resolved and it was therefore a constitutional piece of legislation with a new decision. That was the only difference that existed, the difference with the administrative plenary. But there are also several differences in this legislation. The most important thing is that the central feature of Law 4512, i.e. the balancing of the public interest with the private initiative, is abolished. This aim was for the institution to function effectively. This balance is now being circumvented by turning the plateau to the benefit of individuals. In particular, for ombudsman training bodies under this draft law, negative enlargement is introduced to private individuals. The risks posed by not establishing strict criteria for the establishment of intermediaries training bodies are very serious. You must not ignore them, but instead you must foresee them and prevent them. It is clear that the introduction of compulsory mediation in our lives has opened up a new market for professionals. This means that there must be guarantees of serious and reliable education. European Union law enables Member States to impose restrictions, but also strict conditions for the establishment and functioning of training providers. The introduction of other private bodies in case (c) of Article 22(1), which certainly did not exist in Law 4512, abolishes any institutional control over the training bodies of intermediaries. It completely opens up this sensitive sector to private individuals. The argument for the judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union is unsatisfactory because that judgment concerns the previous law, Law 3898/2010. Already under Law 4512, the field was widened with regard to training centres, providing for a number of forms to be set up, and for the first time introduced public universities into training bodies. There was no reason, therefore, to introduce individuals into the process of training ombudsmen. With the proposed provision, training providers will have as prerequisites the establishment and operation of a low quality assurance, quality control and control function. It is obvious both with legal and logical arguments that there should be a limit and a safeguard as to who can draw up ombudsmen. Alternatively, and if you insist on providing privately for training providers, we propose that a minimum guarantee be set up to set up a large amount of capital, as well as adequate and large building infrastructure, combined with the conditions for the authorisation of type 2 CEDSIBIMs. We are also proposing that the civil liability insurance agreement should be increased. The conditions for a trainer to be considered as under Article 22(6) should also be cumulative and not diagnosed. I would mention here that it is good that our proposal does not make any distinctions for judges, pensioners and that there is no distinction between training providers in terms of trainers and their qualifications. As far as associations of intermediaries are concerned, we highlight the risks that may arise from the absence of strict rules on their establishment and operation. The lack of rules will cause a great deal of trouble that the hand of the market, of course, cannot solve. Initially, these associations, consisting of a number of professionals of different specialties, are likely to act as quasi-legal corporations and provide legal advice and services to citizens of doubtful quality and validity. Thus, citizens' rights are being jeopardised. In the form of a package of services, there is a risk that they will fish customers, without guaranteeing the services provided and without being subject to any supervision by a competent body, as is the case with law firms which are subject to strict control. This means that the ombudsman must not be dependent on employers' interests, nor should he be accountable to any supervisor. If the independence of the ombudsmen is not absolute, they will not succeed in inspiring confidence and the institution will decline too quickly. We are, at least, proposing that these associations of persons should be governed proportionately by the provisions on legal companies. It would also be a good thing if they were to be declared to the Central Mediation Committee to be subject to disciplinary action under an explicit provision. We also propose that you legislate to fully integrate the European Code of Conduct for Mediation Ombudsmen and Providers - what is reflected in Article 20 as associations of persons - as defined in No. CEPEJ (2018) 24 of the competent European Commission, adapted, of course, to the Greek legal system. I shall refer to some specific points in the bill concerning the visibility and advertising of ombudsmen. We propose that the rule of the Code of Laws should be applied proportionately in order to preserve the validity of the institution as an alternative way of resolving disputes. I believe that we all agree on this - a clear and strict framework which leaves no room for misinterpretation. The possibility of advertising should therefore fall within such a regulatory framework and not within the freedom of the private sector. As far as legal aid is concerned, which is ultimately part of the mandatory initial session, we propose providing for a specific fee for the Ombudsman to be provided in accordance with the procedure with the provisions of legal aid. Submission of applicants and persons receiving legal assistance to mediation serves both the same and the same information about the institution. The speed of handling and resolving cases will ease the beneficiaries of legal aid more quickly, reduce the court plates, speed up the payment of lawyers' fees. The parties by definition to legal aid are impossible, and that is why they should be given the opportunity through the mandatory initial session to know the mediation, to follow the procedure and to resolve their dispute at the least cost. It is also proposed that a special register of legal aid agents should be kept at the request of them, of course, similar to that of lawyers. Finally, procedurally, the applicant could be given legal aid to appoint the Ombudsman in the decision to appoint the lawyer, the curator and the notary. For the local jurisdiction of the compulsory original session, we propose that there be provision per court of first instance at the headquarters of the Court of First Instance and with the possibility of extending to the district of appeal. Of course, it must also be taken into account for islands. One issue is that there should be a specific provision for complex trials, as there is a risk that one of the parties might use the mandatory session of paradoxically, so that the institution is affected, but there is also legal uncertainty. In closing the initial suggestion, I would like to say that it is positive to accept the proposals that the Minister mentioned on specific articles, such as those relating to the setting-up of the central mediation committee, the maintenance of the incompatibility, the restriction of the possibility of involving retired and honorary judges, and the obligation that the title of a foreign university be equal to Greek EEI rather than equivalent. That's important. Finally, we repeat that there is no obvious reason for a different date of initiation of the different procedures. This forecast will create more problems than direct and overall problems. We should bear in mind the legal certainty and the objections to the audience or the proposals made in actions brought by lawyers. The best solution, therefore, will be to start implementing everyone at the beginning of the court year and, if that is not possible, to set a date for the start of all proceedings. Mr Minister, ladies and gentlemen, today we welcome to the plenary of the Greek Parliament the introduction of a draft law on an extremely important alternative procedure for resolving disputes, mediation. Let us not forget, after all, that the mediation process, a leading real reform for the area of Greek justice, was essentially introduced for the first time by Law 3898/2010 by the then government of PASOK. Intermediation at international level is an acceptable, effective, rapid and qualitative process. It is a process aimed at a sustainable and beneficial mutual settlement of private disputes. And if something makes it so important for the Greek legal system, it ends up with a process of consensus and understanding between the two parties, the cultivation - if you wish - of a new social model. It does not replace justice, it does not cancel justice, it does not cancel justice, ladies and gentlemen. On the contrary, it complements it, it improves it by making the number one key to the decommissioning of the courts, so for the speedier and better administration of justice, which is the number one problem, the burden of the billboards on Greek justice. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u325	Thank you very much, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, today we are called on to discuss the draft law introduced by the Ministry of Justice entitled "Measure in civil and commercial matters, further harmonisation of Greek legislation with the provisions of Directive 2008/52 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 21 May". It is the attempt of the New Democracy Government to restore mediation and, indeed, compulsory mediation in a wide range of cases. It is an effort launched by PASOK in 2010 with L.3898 and SYRIZA has come to strengthen with the Contonis Law, Law No. 4512/2018. It cannot be surprising that the New Democracy, in essence, now reintroduces a piece of legislation that it itself voted against when it was in the position of Officer counter-population. What has changed since then and has made New Democracy defend the bill? What changed beyond her taking over the country's governance? For the previous law, this 2018, there were, among other things, doubts expressed by the Scientific Service of the Parliament concerning the breach of the right to effective judicial protection guaranteed by our Constitution in Article 20, but also by the European Convention on Human Rights and the Charter of Fundamental Rights. As MERA25, we are positive towards efforts that will ease the workload of the courts, while at the same time reducing the time of resolving a dispute. But we cannot stand positive against the government’s attempt to do so. Let us look more closely at the bill and the articles against which we cannot stand critics, since they are in direct contrast with our own ideological framework and our view of justice. As we have said since the first meeting of the committee responsible, we are opposed to this bill, which effectively restores Law 4512 which was considered unconstitutional by the administrative plenary session of the Ice Bay. It was a decision that, along with all the reactions it caused in judicial and legal circles, led to the withdrawal of the order of compulsory mediation. In more detail, first of all, with Article 3, we have broadened the scope of the mediation institution so that civil or commercial cases of a national or cross-border nature can be dealt with either in the present or in the future, which the directive does not provide and is indeed an originality for our country. Thus, almost all cases will have to pass through the compulsory mediation procedure, while only the cases of the pacifist courts remain out of the process. They are therefore included in family affairs, those which fall within the competence of a single and multiple court, as well as all those for which there is written agreement between the parties provided for in the mediation clause, but with the exception of the specific institution in which a party is the public or an OTA or a legal person governed by public law, as well as those relating to consumer matters which, if applied, may have had a positive effect. Because, as the Minister told us in committee, it is not provided for in the directive. A little further, in Article 5, in particular, in the sixth paragraph, it is stated that if the dispute is finally brought to court because of the failure of mediation, neither the Ombudsman nor the lawyers can be considered as witnesses, as they cannot produce evidence from mediation. It seems here that the author of the bill forgets that there is no possibility of proving any possibility of finding an out-of-court solution, which is the main purpose of mediation, too. How will it be proved, if no evidence is provided, that every effort has been made more and that the procedure has been completed and that we must therefore be brought to the court? Article 7 reintroduces the provision of Law 4512 on the imposition of a penalty payment to any party not coming to the initial compulsory session, in a framework of between EUR 100 and 500, increasing it from its corresponding provision of Law 4512. Thus, the total cost of the procedure is increased, which is in complete contrast with the operative part of the decision of the Administrative Plenary Assembly of the Ice Arena, which requires an inexpensive procedure. A little further, in Article 10, the central mediation committee is described. It is worth noting here that we have a reduction of thirteen Members, which is an increase of eight anticipated by the Council. In the case of the United Kingdom, it is for the national court to determine whether the conditions laid down in Article 5 of Regulation (EEC) No 3898 are fulfilled. Two of these thirteen members are required to be teachers of higher education institutions in our country, only one being required to have been a professor of law school, without any provision being made for the latter. In addition, the fourth point of the first paragraph of that Article provides as members of the CED for three representatives of the Ministry of Justice by the officials at the central office or by the officials of posted lawyers in the political office of a member of the Ministry's political leadership. The Minister has committed itself - and we are expected to see it - that the number of these members will be reduced to two of three. However, we have not yet received a reply to the question we asked about how the members of the CED are to be selected as provided for in the first four cases of this paragraph. We also expect to see the ministerial decision on the definition of the JRC and to demonstrate that the participation of the members in it will be unpaid. Although we still wonder why some of the Ministry's permanent employees shouldn't be chosen. On the contrary, we see the possibility of postponing posts to posted officials from the political offices of this leadership by extending the possibility - beyond the Minister's office - to that of the Secretary-General and the Secretary-General. In Article 12, the qualifications of ombudsmen are described as essential to the possession of an EIO degree and to the attendance of an eighty-hour seminar organised by a certified training body. Within eighty hours and having taken a few civil, Family and Commercial Law courses, it will be possible to become accredited as a mediator with the aim of concluding a final solution or trying to resolve disputes and disputes that cover a wide range of cases. We think it's not enough eighty hours, which will appear later, when we have countless failures to take the court path. This will ultimately increase the cost of the "competent" which will be borne by both incomplete mediation and a judicial dispute. At the same time, ladies and gentlemen, this bill provides for a fee of EUR 50 for the first compulsory session that is equally distributed between the parties, while a lawyer is required. During the first session, the ombudsman does not even enter into the substance of the case unless he examines the possibility to resolve out-of-court proceedings and decides whether mediation can continue or not. Then, in the second case, his remuneration is around eighty euros per hour, which is once again shared between the parties in accordance with Article 18. In any event, everything indicates that it will greatly increase the costs to be borne by each party. If we think that we are living at a time when many are unable to cover the amount that they are given, even for the first session, 25 euros, and especially if we are talking about labour law cases and disputes between workers and employers, it is extremely likely that the workers will leave the case. If we add, in the difficult economic position, to the fact that, in fact, workers never have the same negotiating power and power as employers, then it is almost certain that we will have many mediation efforts where the weakest part will simply resign. Articles 22-25 describe training bodies, the licensing of natural and legal persons wishing to obtain a licence, so that they can certify an intermediary. It can only surprise individuals, i.e. individuals, to open up a centre that attests to ombudsmen. What assurances are there, ladies and gentlemen, so that every city does not fill up with training centres in a short time and does not even have the necessary framework for protecting the quality of the services provided? This will jeopardise both the quality of mediation and the quality of the ombudsmen. Unfortunately, the neoliberal spirit that it breaths and this piece of legislation is in complete contrast with what we represent. We would support an effort that would alleviate the court bottles, but not the way the Ministry of Justice is attempting it with this law. We consider this to be an attempt in the right direction, but in the wrong way. Indeed, several countries have introduced the institution of mediation into their legislation with beneficial results for their judges, but also with a final reduction in the time to resolve disputes. A characteristic example is Italy, which, after having moved between voluntary and compulsory mediation, concluded in 2013 the provision for a compulsory session, which benefited the courts of the neighbour. Unfortunately, however, in our country the concept of extrajudicial dispute resolution and mediation is still premature. When, at the same time, we have such legislative efforts, which leave the scope for commercialisation and privatisation free, which are likely to involve anti-constitutional votes, incorporating provisions of Law 4512, and which ultimately increase the cost of a citizen who wants to resort to mediation, the opportunity to strengthen the credibility of the institution is unfortunately being removed. [[At this point, the fourth Vice-President of Parliament, Mr DIMITRIOS VITSAS, takes over the Presidential Chair.]] In conclusion, I would like to point out that it is a good question that, although the provisions of the previous law return, the Ice Arrow's administrative plenary delivered an opinion on the constitutionality of the draft in question at the request of the Minister of Justice. In particular, the forty-three members of the Administrative Assembly unanimously considered the arrangements of the bill unconstitutional, which has sincerely given us a surprise. Ladies and gentlemen, the promotion of citizens in opaque violations and the creation of a new profession, which will leave numerous young, unemployed graduates of chemistry, who will be exploited as a cheap workforce, without anyone finally knowing what connections and what economic relationships they may hide behind every mediation centre, find us perpendicularly opposing. We cannot in any way consent to the tradition of justice in the private sector and in the business sector. After all this, we can only vote against this bill at first and look after the individual articles. For the amendment, I will be placed later. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u335	Thank you, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, we lawyers and, above all, our colleagues who are fighting for lawyers, but above all the parties represented in the courts, represented in the courts, experience and experience the slow and psycho-corruption process in Greece every day. And some of our clients, by the parties, may never have had time to collect the price of justice in a variety of ways. With the prevailing conditions of judiciary in Greece and simple civil and commercial cases, from the submission of a lawsuit to the first instance, at least six hundred 10 days are required on average, i.e. twenty-two months, according to an investigation by the Society of Judiciary Studies in 2016 and since then, I can assure you that they have not changed much. In this period, apparently, there are no delays, such as delays, which can really eject the period of service in several more hundred days or even a few months additional. These months, I would dare say the years of delay, may be crucial for maintaining commercial cooperation for the operation of a business. Needless to mention, of course, the demand for large sums of money as a cost for judicial costs when one-way recourse to the judicial route to resolve disputes is chosen. An alternative to these time-consuming procedures comes to offer out-of-court mediation, which, as we know, has already been in place for decades in the United States, the United Kingdom, Australia, to resolve urban and commercial disputes, with a success rate of more than 75%. In recent years, this practice has recorded strong growth in the rest of Europe, particularly in Italy, with two hundred and fifty thousand cases closed by the method of mediation, France and Germany. So at international level this process is not just an acceptable process, an effective, rapid and qualitative, but is extremely attractive because of its important advantages, which is saving time and money. Ladies and gentlemen, the framework of the institution of the extrajudicial mediation has been highly concise and accurately attributed to a few lines by a prominent member of the judiciary, the former Prime Minister, now Vice-President of the Government and honorary President of the Council of State, Mr Panagiotis Pikrammenos, who said: "The methods of alternative dispute resolution are not in competition with official state justice, do not replace the courts, do not help to upgrade them. The administration of justice must adapt and go hand in hand with modern life, the current problems of society, economic development, technology in the era of the fourth industrial revolution." With this bill, ladies and gentlemen, the government is attempting to fulfil one of the main obligations of the country vis-à-vis the institutions, but also to Greek citizens both in the context of reforms and in the context of the enhanced surveillance programme, which, of course, the previous government had committed itself to introducing mediation. This practice, having an initial spirit optional in its implementation, made its appearance in 2008 with European Parliament Directive 2008/52, which was incorporated into Greek legislation in 2010, with Law 3398/2010. However, the updated Law 4512/2018, on the one hand, was judged to be a constitutional year by Marios Igos, on the other hand, by a decision of the Court of Justice of the European Union, on the other hand, the criteria laid down by Greek legislation for the possibility of setting up bodies for the training of intermediaries by a natural or legal person, were considered to be particularly restrictive. It is therefore called upon today that the present Government be brought into line with the judgments of the Supreme Court by legislating again. At the same time, the Ministry of Justice aims to transform the mediation process from an optional institution into a conscious choice of citizens. This will result in the removal of the judiciary so that, over time, both the courts and the administration of justice can operate at a faster and more efficient rate. It is a common place that if justice, which is a pillar of our democracy, our democratic urban culture, does not work, then a number of problems begin to appear at the forefront, so that they are a brake on the development of the economic and social life of the country. The bill clearly defines the factors of the procedure, their roles and responsibilities, particularly the Ombudsman, who, beyond his scientific training and the ability to carry out his duties successfully, must be independent and neutral. At the same time, it emerges with this bill - and rightly so - the role of the lawyer with the obligation of his presence from the first meeting, which is the only one required. Of course, it is not only enough to legislate properly, but the similar culture in society also needs to be developed in order to promote and consolidate the model on which the Government's initiative was based. The extrajudicial resolution of disputes could be a key to the decommissioning of the courts and therefore to the more rapid administration of justice. However, we must stand by the basic requirement that this should be achieved, that we all work systematically and methodically with a storm of information and information for the citizens, in order to make it the conscience of the Greek people, the Greek society that it is worth trying to resolve a civil dispute without recourse to the court. It is a bill other than the others, a piece of legislation that comes to cure problematic arrangements of the past and to push society into the choice of a specific alternative for which it intends, the purpose and the wish of the government and the ministry is to be a useful tool in resolving disputes, among individuals, among citizens, by dismantling justice, our assured judicial system both in volume and in money and in time. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u336	Thank you, Mrs. Macrie. The New Democracy Parliamentary Representative, Mr Bougas, has the floor for twelve minutes.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u337	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the government by the Minister of Justice is recommending a bill which can be part of the resolution of the private. It is well known to all of us - and it has been discussed many times in the House - that the great problem facing us today, but also always, Greek justice is the delay in its awarding, which has caused - and I think you all know that - many convictions against our country by the European Court of Human Rights for violating the reasonable time for the political, criminal and administrative justice in our country. To address this problem, one could propose - and was proposed by the Greek speaker - an increase in the number of judges. You know, it's always been a request from associations of Judges and Prosecutors, the increase in the organic positions of judges. The answer, however, is: On the basis of the data and indeed of the European Commission's recent report on the effectiveness of justice, published in October 2018, the number of judges has increased overall in Greece by 25% for the period 2014-2016. In Greece they account for 25.8 professional judges in a hundred thousand inhabitants, with an average internationally of 21.5. In other words, we have a ratio of judges in relation to the population of the largest in the world. Despite the great increase, however, of the judges and at a time of difficulty for the country, the problem is still there. The increase in their number cannot be the only solution and, of course, it cannot be continued at all times. We are therefore obliged to seek solutions outside the judiciary. Alternative ways of resolving private disputes include mediation, i.e. the process in which the parties with the assistance of a third and an independent person, the Ombudsman, who has obtained specific training, identify the issues of the dispute between them, investigate the alternatives to resolve them and attempt to reach an agreement satisfying their real interests. The constitutional basis of this procedure is the provision of Article 5 of our Constitution. Of course, if you look at private law, it's Article 361 of the Civil Code. Mediation is not private justice, as some speakers have said, the Ombudsman is certainly not and does not replace the judge. Let me take a historical regression to make the role of mediation more understandable internationally. The institution of mediation began in the 1970s and began to spread to the United States of America. At European level, Directive 52/2008 on mediation in civil and commercial matters is important. In our country for the first time, the institution of mediation in 2001 was presented at one of the most important and most extensive scientific conferences of International Judicial Law, held in Athens by the University of Athens Law School and the Institute of Economic Studies. The first Greek legislator with mediation comes to the year 2005, when at the initiative of the then Minister of Justice - he was the Resurrection Papaliguras Minister of Justice at the time - a legislative committee was set up to reform the Code of Civil Procedure with a clear suggestion to its members - President, the Scientific Committee was Professor Klamaris - to provide specific provisions regulating mediation. Then, on the basis of the Community directive I mentioned, a separate legislative committee was set up under the auspices of Papalegura in order to draw up the draft law incorporating the Community directive into the Greek legal order. As a result of this important legal and political work, which preceded it, it is the fact that Greece was one of the first countries of the European Union, which incorporated the directive with No.3898/2010. Unfortunately, however, the efforts of the legislator, both by Law No.3898 and by later Law No. 4512, to introduce the mediation institution have failed, as many, unfortunately, have failed to do so far. These reasons for the poor performance of the institutions, which could really contribute and contribute to the decommissioning of the courts, must be looked at in particular in the absence of a spirit of conciliation, which is unfortunately diffuse in Greek society, of course, along with the attitude of the Greek lawyers, in particular, who discourage the parties in the search for such solutions. However, I think it is time to tackle mediation not as a typical obstacle that simply moves the start of legal proceedings, as so far many lawyers have taken it, but as a constructive process, from which everyone can win. This is because the difference is resolved more quickly, with less money, in less time. It shall also be resolved on the basis of a procedure specified by the parties and not strictly in accordance with the rules of procedure. However, the most important thing - particularly in commercial matters - is that, because of the mutual nature of the mediation institution, the parties retain the possibility of continuing their cooperation. In the candidate ombudsmen, for a better understanding of the institution and the above benefits the following example is usually mentioned, which is typical of those who have received mediator training. It is a real example, and I would ask you to allow me to mention it in the Chamber. In 1990, a ship from Israel sailed to the port of London, loaded with oranges. There were two companies certified and equipped with the relevant documents for claiming the consignment. They both appealed to the courts to resolve their dispute. Very soon, however, these companies were faced with two real problems. The first was that oranges began rotting and the second was that they had to make very high steps for the ship's stay at the port. These companies appealed to an ombudsman, who was the first to ask for the shipment of each of these two companies. One, then, replied that he wanted the oranges to make jam from their peel and the other that he wanted their juice. A solution has been found immediately, without either of them losing. Both parties have been completely satisfied with the mediation appeal and of course their aims have been fully achieved. Speed in resolving the dispute, satisfying both parties, avoiding burdens on the judiciary, this is the third parameter. The judicial system is being unloaded from the cases, which are resolved in the mediation. Ladies and gentlemen, this draft law establishes a comprehensive, structured system of mediation rules distinguishing it from previous laws. Indicatively and in relation to the previous law, I would point out the following: compulsory mediation in Article 6 is clearly regulated, in contrast to voluntary mediation in Article 5. In the previous piece of legislation, there was confusion in the cases of compulsory mediation and the consequences of any failure to show a party were not clearly expressed. The number of ombudsmen participating in the Central Mediation Committee from two to three is also increasing, in the presence of which the work of the Committee will be facilitated, since no one in practice is better aware of the institution than the ombudsmen themselves. Also, another issue, the issue of remuneration, is now well formulated in this bill. The minimum remuneration for the compulsory initial session is EUR 50, shared by the two parties, since of course the parties have not reached another written agreement, and for each subsequent session the remuneration amounts to EUR 80. It should be noted here that the previous form of the remuneration law created a great deal of confusion, as it provided for two different amounts of remuneration, some EUR 170 and elsewhere EUR 150, and ultimately was one of the main reasons, which led in essence to the suspension of the law. The application of compulsory mediation, i.e. from 15-12020 in respect of disputes dealt with in the ordinary course of the multi-member procedure, from 15-32020 in respect of family differences other than those excluded, and from 15-5-2020 in respect of disputes brought before the ordinary court of First Instance. Minister, I would ask you here to consider this: because, as you know, the competence of the Single Court of First Instance begins with the sum of EUR 20,000, it would be appropriate, at least in the first phase, to increase the amount of compulsory mediation, i.e. 30 000, which I think is reasonable, and to start with this amount the compulsory mediation. There's a suggestion. If the parties have other proposals, we can talk about it. I think we might find a common place in this. In this way, the legislature aims to deal with any malfunctions. The evaluation is also planned after the end of the first two years of practical application of the compulsoryity of the first mediation session, which is very important and is linked to the rules of good legislation. I would particularly like to point out, before I finish today's speech, ladies and gentlemen, the fact that mediation results in concrete results. And what are these? When either party appeals to the court, it usually seeks legal certainty, which is made by means of a judicial decision, in the majority of cases. The main features of a judicial decision are legality and enforceability. If the parties also reach a conclusion on what they agree to draw up a report which is submitted, as provided for in the draft law under discussion, these two outcomes sought by the parties may be obtained, and it is doubtful whether, following a long-term judicial procedure, they succeed. This is because there is a 'judgment' in dispute, namely that it is not allowed for the same dispute, which has been resolved by mediation, to address any party from any party to the court and there is, of course, the possibility of obtaining enforcement, that is to say, that it may, by means of enforcement procedures, be enforced in practice, which will have been lodged with the Court of First Instance and has become, in accordance with the procedure laid down in Articles 904 et seq. of the subsequent instrument of enforcement. This is done by a particularly significant reduction in the costs of proceedings, because if one chooses to bring proceedings, which will finalise and be able to take a decision after six, seven years, it is directly obliged to pay the judicial notice, which amounts to about 1% of the claimant and after the final hearing, so that he can take 'the census', i.e. the document which will enable him to fulfil his claim, will also have to pay the charge. So all this entails a very high cost which, if anyone resorts to the mediation process, gets away with it, there are no legal costs and even so high. Ladies and gentlemen, the government with the draft law under discussion demonstrates its willingness to introduce rules of extrajudicial resolution into the Greek legal order. This political will of the government must be confirmed by voting in favour of the bill and by aligning it with European requirements. I think it is a bill of good law, which will make it possible to solve important problems and to remove a number of disharmonies, which unfortunately have been tempting Greek justice for many decades. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u349	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the bill on which we are called upon to place ourselves today is the second attempt to integrate Directive 2008/52 of the European Parliament of 21 May 2018. The first attempt was made with Law 3898/2010 and has not worked. The direction of the directive is to enable citizens to take action in an alternative way, in the context of a friendly settlement, with a view to simplifying the administration of justice and, ultimately, to speeding up the administration of justice. But here, there is a paradox. Since 2001 with Law No 2915/2001 and until now, at the rate of one to two laws a year, there has been an attempt to speed up policy, sometimes criminal and sometimes administrative justice. Approximately twenty laws on speeding up or simplifying its administration to justice in eighteen years. This is a negative worldwide record of the Greek political system, of course. Nevertheless, the large proportion of citizens, rightly, are not satisfied either with the speed or the quality of justice. In the legal order several times attempted to speed up the administration of justice, sometimes by setting deadlines and lastly by prescribing sentences and pleas, sometimes by introducing violations of legal remedies, at times by assigning more cases to the courts and at times by establishing alternative ways of regulating disputes. There are now five ways of non-judicial resolution of private disputes: Arbitration, compromise settlement, attempted compromise, judicial mediation and mediation. They have not worked either because they are too expensive, because they are time-consuming or because they are lacking in clarity. So we are regulating mediation today without having wondered why none of these ways of extrajudicial dispute settlement have worked. Each government is bombing Parliament by a storm of legislation, and this is projected as a productive government project. So we end up with evil and pluralism, with ambiguity, conflicting provisions and without a systematic approach. This is also the big issue, the absence of a simple and clear legal system. Instead of thinking about how to change these distortions, instead of increasing the funds for justice, instead of planning to increase the organic positions of judges and the recruitment of a sufficient number of judges, instead of supporting and shielding justice, we are essentially legislating to prevent the citizen from resorting to justice. Today, with the bill, we are inspiring the concept of 'private justice'. This is also our leading disagreement with the institution of mediation. No individual can grant compulsory law in a forced form, as was done before the French Revolution, on feudalism. We are standing in the democratic need of the principle of natural justice, of the person with functional and personal independence, which is generally legitimised in advance to judge a particular person and a specific case, according to the law and the Constitution. Perhaps the judge's greatest independence is a way of speeding up justice. Today, however, with the draft law we confess to the failure to speed up the administration of the judiciary of Law4335/2015. You claim that you regulate the slow pace of justice by mediation, which has already been introduced into the Greek legal system nine years ago with Law 3898/2010 and which has failed. The institution that has failed, we seek to make it compulsory'regulating' it, today, in some procedures, tomorrow to all. The weaker economic citizens will also face additional costs. In mediation they will not feel security, since the Ombudsman will be any scientific expertise, without compulsory circular legal studies. Can the few seminars of certification inspire citizens' confidence in the fullness of the ombudsman? Ladies and gentlemen, there are no adequate guarantees for the citizen's impartiality, impartiality and objectivity. There shall be no guarantee of transparency with the absence of the obligation to make public and the specific and in-depth statement of reasons. The judgments published and the case-law are a safe guide and the lawyer who will advise his client about his actions and the judge in disputes for which he will rule. There will be no such information in mediation. You're probably trying to solve an unpleasant situation. Many young scientists, ombudsmen, have no professional matter, although since 2010 they have paid very large sums for their education and certification in intermediation centres with few conditions of establishment and operation. Instead of checking these procedures and asking for responsibility today, are we legalising them, ladies and gentlemen? So, beyond my vertical opposition to the bill, I also find some serious shortcomings. I will not say that they are intentional shortcomings. The law, for example, exhaustively regulates the issues of course after the failure of mediation, how the practice of mediation will be carried out, the effect of mediation on limitations and other deadlines. However, a key issue is not regulated. For what clear reasons the citizen can turn against the practical effect of mediation, if, for example, he finds out, after all, that he has been deceived. Why is it not for a specialised court to judge differences in mediation with transparency and guarantees? Will mediation differences be resolved again by mediation? Should mediation be a fully voluntary process? In the way that we are currently regulating mediation, justice is not speeding up, nor is it qualitatively upgraded. What is certain is the violent deflation of the law by day-to-day lawyers, whose material is, of course, falling, while also under pressure from the obligation to pay excessive social security premiums, as well as the obligation to pay a high tax, such as all self-employed scientists. And in parentheses, high taxation for self-employed scientists goes on with the new tax bill you submitted, while you did not lighten their heavy insurance burdens, in total contrast to your election announcements. The conclusion, therefore, is that access to the very few hegemonicly paid lawyers, who would award justice, will only have one rich oligarchy. Justice will be an oligarchal luxury where and whenever it suits the mighty. In other cases, mediation will be the private justice of the few who seek to resolve their differences with the weaker, away from the lights of publicity. No annoying details, no trials, no judges, no lawyers. It is certain that this is not called justice. It is clear that this process does not serve democracy, ladies and gentlemen. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u351	Thank you, Mr President. Mr Ministers, ladies and gentlemen, Greece is among the countries with most convictions from the European Court of Human Rights for delays in the administration of justice. This problem is not new, it is time-consuming, as are the fragmentary reforms, which usually solve a problem in the justice system, but at the same time create other new ones. Why, though, is that so? Greece does not lack a sufficient number of judges, as is not lacking in competent judges. The Greek judges also respond to their duties in extremely difficult circumstances and, in most cases, manage to issue in the nearest year the highest possible number of decisions, without a discount on the quality of legal crises. However, the problem remains as much reform as it is in the judiciary, no matter how speedy the procedures are by adopting new regulations in civil, criminal and administrative matters. Because, ladies and gentlemen, the real cause of the problem is that the number of cases pending before the courts remains too large. And that is because, contrary to what is happening in the developed countries of Western Europe and America, in Greece, we have not yet become acquainted with the alternative forms of dispute resolution. We remain at institutional level, but also as a society, committed to the resolution of disputes by the head of the committee. At mental level above all we prefer the judge to impose a solution - even unfavourable to us - rather than sit on the other side at the same table to find a mutually acceptable solution. The extrajudicial resolution of disputes remains at hybrid level. Its different forms have either not been legislated, fragmented, or legislated in such a way that it becomes impossible to implement in practice. The first and perhaps the most important form of alternative dispute resolution is mediation. Intermediation is not appropriate for all types of cases, nor can it be applied everywhere with the same success. It is appropriate, however, for differences with purely or primarily financial, economic or property, such as most civil cases, commercial affairs or even family differences, such as nutrition and custody of children. In these cases it is also about mediation with the bill we are debating today. Why is this bill necessary? But precisely because in relation to what I said earlier, mediation is a characteristic institution which was introduced into the Greek legal order in such a way that it does not apply in practice. For almost a decade now, thousands of ombudsmen have been trained and certified throughout the entire Greek territory and yet, so far, very little mediation has taken place throughout Greece. Ladies and gentlemen, the original mediation law of 2010 has failed miserably, because you cannot introduce a completely new institution into the Greek legal order and not provide for ways in which it operates in practice. You cannot legislate anything new and at the same time satisfy all those interests that benefit from the old. And after so many years with no effect, the SYRIZA - ANEL government passed new provisions on mediation in 2018. This law could have been in the right direction because, in fact, it had to introduce the obligation of mediation as a front of the procedure before the courts for these reasons: to familiarize citizens and lawyers, with the new institution, in the hope, at the same time, that a large number of cases would be resolved at this stage. In order to save people time and money and finally get rid of the system of awarding the great number of cases. In order to speed up the resolution of disputes at all levels, both out-of-court cases to be resolved in the context of mediation and in judicial matters through the justice system. But this law has failed miserably. Its arrangements, as a result of the misrepresentation which characterised the previous government, were considered unconstitutional by the administrative plenary session of the Ice Bay. The law was suspended and the Court of Justice of the European Union ruling that the restrictions of the previous legislative regime, with regard to the training bodies of ombudsmen, were contrary to Union law, was then adopted. All this, ladies and gentlemen, today we come to deal with it, cure the sufferings of the past, and finally give the Greek legal order a modern piece of legislation for the implementation of mediation in practice. Specific categories of cases will be subject to the initial mediation session, but with strict conditions and guarantees and with full respect for the right to legal protection afterwards. This is why, contrary to the previous law of the Government of SYRIZA - ANEL, the administrative plenary of the Ice Bay approved - and even unanimously - as a constitutional bill, this bill proposed by the Government of New Democracy, a bill which, with the support of Greek society, lawyers and ombudsmen, is expected to contribute substantially to the rapid and effective resolution of private disputes. For all these reasons, I urge you to vote in favour. Thank you.
