ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u388	Thank you very much, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, I shall refer first to the bill and then to the amendment. So we are discussing today a bill in which the government has given special weight to communication. The Ministry presents compulsory mediation in private affairs as something that is necessary for the law of the Union itself and as an economic and rapid solution. Members of the New Democracy speak of a tool that will harness the Greek law and release the bottles. In the media, too, the Italian example is often heard. So all this is invoked by the government rather - much as data and post offices, which cannot be called into question. Is that really so? First, I shall invoke the famous Directive 52/2008. In the introductory paragraph 8, the directive says that it should apply only to cross-border disputes, that is, when, for example, a Greek supplier has differences with a French customer. In other cases it says that Member States are not required, but they may apply those provisions, not that they are required. So transferring the model of the directive to our reality is not an obligation at all, but an option. This results without any margin of doubt from the directive itself. So let us make it clear to the citizens that compulsory private mediation in civil and commercial matters does not bear the European Union's seal, but the seal of the government itself, and of course Law 4512/2018 brings the SYRIZA seal. The first conclusion, therefore, is that compulsory mediation does not require us by the European Union. I now come to the issue of bicommunalism. It makes me a real impression that this has been heard by fellow lawyers. In 2019, a model, a stereotype that has not been confirmed for a long time, given the crisis, is being reproduced. On today's day for what bipartisan Greece are we talking about? Bicoloria, as we know, is a luxurious sport, which requires economic power. Who, then, are the money - especially this day - to be a coward? Even we see it in the criminal courts, where there is a risk of imprisonment, and yet we know all the lawyers who have the relevant experience that they are mostly filled up by the authoritarian appointment, because the defendants are unable to afford even their own defence. We even see it in divorces where most are consensual about disputes. So from bicommunication, do the bottles fill up, or, for example, from applications for the debts and protection of the first place? The primary courtrooms are full of them? Those who are in the field and practice the profession see how the courts are moving and how many just lawyers have representations. We're talking about 60% to 70% of lawyers who in one year may not have a single show in court. So let's go on to the issue of international good practice, which we invoke. Even in Europe, mediation is almost everywhere voluntary and optional. Exemptions are Italy and Malta in some family differences and Austria in few cases. The conclusion, therefore, is that such extensive compulsory mediation is not just an international practice, but is a very limited exception. It is just your own broad model which will be a pan-European originality or an irregularity with the blessings of BSE and insurance companies. The issue of costs has been analysed several times. I won't insist. However, it follows from that that conclusion that mediation is not an economic solution which ensures comfortable access to the average citizen, because at a monthly salary of 600 euros it cannot be considered as an affordable amount of some EUR 1,000 that is estimated to cost the mediation in a case, even moderately difficult. Furthermore, if mediation eventually fails, the citizen will be called and paid more and waited longer, since the cost along with any penalty will then be added to the costs and the procedure will be delayed further. You allow persons who have no legal qualifications to be certified and acting as intermediaries. This is the paradox of the bill. Citizens, in other words, will address issues with legal implications to an Ombudsman, who may be a graduate of Physical Education, Geology, Theology. The reference to these fields is indicative. My goal is not to degrade any cognitive objects. Here, then, we will have an ombudsman who will be called upon to deal with legal issues in which the legislatures themselves often disagree, there is controversial and divided case law, even in the courts. How reliable, then, will an Ombudsman who is called upon to regulate a situation when present lawyers will much better know the subject than he is? It is therefore completely incompatible and inappropriate to deal with such cases by a professional who has no serious knowledge of the law, and for whom the eighty hours of training are very little to gain this knowledge. Nor is there any point in telling us that the interested parties will be able to choose a legal ombudsman if they want to do so, and so there is no problem, because if all interested parties finally make this wise choice for their own security, all the non-legislators who have paid badly to draw up and certify themselves will simply cry at EUR 2 000 and at the hours they have spent. You are also telling us that the citizen does not lose the right to access the natural judge because the court will simply ratify the practical compromise. The judges themselves answer as follows: Either the case will be examined completely formal and superficial - and therefore there will be no guarantee, beyond the Ombudsman's own good faith and credibility - or the judge will in the end examine all the case in order to confirm the final compromise, with the consequence that he has already caused the delay he supposed to prevent mediation. You are also telling us that there is no question of the independence and impartiality of ombudsmen. First of all, where does this come from? What do the judges say about this? If it is involved, for example, in the difference between a bank or an insurance company or another natural or legal person, which is forced by reason of the object of recourse to mediation with a high frequency and therefore there is an increased volume of cases, it is very intense that the temptation for the intermediary ultimately prefers to maintain this part in his clientele, showing the favor and discrimination required in favour of the most powerful party in his decisions. All these points have already emerged in Decision 34/2018 of the Administrative Plenary Assembly of the Ice Bay, which considered the contents of several provisions of the previous law unconstitutional. Here again, we see this paradox: While basically the structure and basic features of this draft law differ little from the previous law, the administrative plenary is now in its new decision to overturn the previous one. There is a complete transformation and there is no unconstitutionalism. I'm not going to get into public criticism. There are simply reasonable and legitimate questions to everyone in this regard. Clearly I do not think there is a reasonable person who prefers the disputes to the agreement. There is therefore no question as to the value and usefulness of mediation in general. This is clearly an extremely attractive way out, which will have advantages both for citizens and for society. It can certainly promote the resolution of climate-consensual disputes in order to avoid tense situations, which, for example, in family differences, have seen poisoning social relations. However, mediation by mediation differs. There are various models and various solutions that are envisaged even in our own law. For example, extrajudicial mediation works when it is based on the sincere will for a mutually acceptable compromise. This sincere will under no circumstances can be the result of compulsion. It depends mainly on the actual potential of stakeholders, along with the nature and difficulty of the dispute. But most importantly, it depends on the incorporation of a similar culture which will allow such institutions to proceed effectively, efficiently and above all in terms of equal treatment of the parties and impartiality. Here, therefore, compulsoryity is even contrary to the very concept of mediation which must be a product of choice rather than a coercion. Instead of precedenting an institution in specific cases where it is seriously likely that it can actually have some tangible and immediate results, the government prefers an extension to almost all civil and commercial matters. Of course, there is another paradox that has already been touched by colleagues: there are differences from the compulsory initial session when the party is the public, the Otta and the legal entities governed by public law. I ask: is this the example of a good culture which the state itself must give its citizens, to oblige him to take part in a process which is in the common interest, but which is ultimately excluded from it? The most important issue, arising from the bill, is also the one causing the greatest anxiety. There is a clear aim here to privatize justice, to favour certain interests. You create a place of entrepreneurship where the state itself traditionally has constitutional authority. This, of course, follows the well-known neoliberal pattern, we deport the public from within and then load it all the suffering and thus appears to the private as a manna from heaven. This soil is intensively cultivated now. We see it in health, education, social security. So now we see it in the field of justice. Unique winners will certainly not the ombudsmen themselves, but the businessmen, mediators, who will ultimately have them as mere basic salary employees, with case-clearing bonuses. But who are the great losers? On the one hand, the economically weak citizens, who will be governed by the well-founded party and a system of mediation of doubtful credibility and impartiality. On the other hand, it is our own state which today indirectly confesss to being weak and unable to provide its citizens with effective judicial protection and thus accepts to grant part a very fundamental pillar to individuals. The question is, why should the judges and lawyers themselves not be specifically trained in this regard, as it is a question of ineffectiveness? In closing my position, I shall refer to the fourth amendment on mortgage rights. On the one hand, you are burdening citizens with increasing their rights and, on the other, you are extending the collection of some of them to private mortgages. This is what you tell us that you are doing so that the mortgages give up part of the increase, 1/4 or 1/8, to support the logistics infrastructure of mortgages and property offices. Firstly, it is not clear what will become the rest and much more of the revenue from the increases, namely 3/4 to 7. What about this? Will the mortgage guards themselves enclose it? Secondly, according to Law 4512/2018, mortgages are already gradually transferred to the new property structure, which will be subject to the Ministry of the Environment. It should be noted that this merger has been under way for two years. It's already been delayed over time. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u392	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the reason I wanted to speak in the debate on this law is precisely because I think it is a huge opportunity to change the conflict resolution map in our country. Ten years after the adoption of the first mediation law, the Ministry of Justice brings to plenary a truly excellent law, proving the strong political will of the government to take steps forward, aligning Greece with modern practices that apply very successfully to tens of legal systems of many countries in the world and at the same time satisfying a demand from the mature Greek society that a large volume of private dispute resolution should be transferred from the courts' halls to the mediation rooms. Ladies and gentlemen, mediation has not come to replace anything, much more justice. She's here to help her. The modern trend, which exists all over the world, is declassification. The mediation law will unload a charged judicial system from thousands of cases. It will give more time to the hearing, make the terms of office of judges and courts more human, speed up the time of judgments. Some have talked about privatization of justice just to create impressions. But mediation, ladies and gentlemen, does not make any decisions. The ombudsmen shall not be judges. There are excellent judges who have this role. In mediation, the decision is in the hands of the parties. Do you know what that means? The social dimension means much less conflict, a new culture of resolution, a less economic burden for the citizen. At a business level, it means a direct solution to thousands of cases, a continuation of the trade relationship between the parties, an economy which does not stop producing GDP at budgetary and institutional level, and a justice that operates more efficiently and at lower cost. Ladies and gentlemen, the high degree of this law is undoubtedly the obligation of the parties to come to an initial meeting with a neutral ombudsman in order to examine the possibility of resolving their dispute by mediation so that they do not go to the courts. I should like to stress at this point that the new law is fully in line with the basic conditions which judge the constitutionality of the compulsory mediation of Article 6 and something which, in a deafening way, confirmed the pre-day opinion of forty-three judges of the Ice Board of Directors. We are finally in the face of a new start with the sealing of everyone, in a really large section, which - let me say that - should have been in Greece for many years now, and is still happening today. With the provision of compulsoryity and no change in the voluntary nature of the procedure and the parties now have the way of a consensual solution through a structured process, a process with prestigious and spectacular statistics on the resolution of cases internationally. It is also extremely good that, with regard to the previous law, the new law considerably widens the scope of the disputes that are subject to compulsory mediation. As is also appropriate to provide for the law on gradual introduction of cases, in order to allow the necessary time to adapt the system to be managed. Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to believe that the legal community - which is at the heart of this process - with lawyers both as ombudsmen and as lawyers, will support the new law in practice, making it part of daily legal practice. Thousands of legal offices around the world provide mediation services and use the institution for millions of cases. It is my strong belief that the new institution will add new material and new possibilities to the profession. I also want to believe that the Greek judges will make use of the provision of the law by calling on thousands of parties to find a solution to mediation. It happens in judiciary systems of dozens of countries, with spectacular results. I am, finally, sure that the Ministry, in cooperation with all market operators, will take advantage of this great opportunity to present the new institution with all the available means, so that mediation is not just used, but to become part of our consciousness and everyday habit. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, before us we have a very modern law and a strong perspective to change the image of dispute resolution procedures. The Ministry did an excellent job, useful legislative improvements that resulted from discussion in committees, with an open spirit of legislating at every stage of the process. In front of us is the great bet of the legal community to fully integrate this institution. I think it's a bet that's going to be won. I am sure this law will be passed by all the groups of Parliament, sending a message that Greece is going through a new era. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u394	Thank you very much, Mr President. I would like to talk about an amendment, with a general number of 100 and a special number 8, concerning the responsibilities of the Securities and Markets Committee for the convening of a general meeting of companies. With these provisions we are equiping the Securities Committee, i.e. the competent body responsible for supervising the Greek capital market, with the possibility of effective intervention in cases where there are phenomena of significant malfunctioning of companies listed in the Greek Stock Exchange. A recent example of the distortion and repetition of the institutional framework for the proper functioning of listed companies is that of FOLLI FOLLIE and its management. This new institutional tool of the Securities Committee, with which we worked closely for this purpose, aims to defend the proper functioning and credibility of the Greek market and thereby defend the interests of investors and strengthen the economy. In particular, the proposed provision provides in exceptional cases only the possibility for the Securities Committee to request a listed company, such as the one I mentioned, the convening of a general meeting of its shareholders in a similar manner of what is currently foreseen for minority shareholders and the company auditor, to request the court to appoint a temporary director of the listed company. It is also about the company, when it is essentially in a lack of administration. These specific forecasts are limited to the extent necessary to defend the proper functioning of the Greek capital market. I want to be quite clear. We act decisively, boldly, efficiently. The provisions do not constitute an intervention in the functioning of the public limited liability company or in the management of corporate affairs, but an action to deal with specific circumstances such as FOLLI FOLLIE, for the purpose of protecting and ensuring the smooth operation of the capital market, which is a public interest reason. I note, moreover, that, as a consequence of limiting the measure to the absolute necessary extent, listed companies, such as banks, which are already under the supervision of the SSM and the Bank of Greece, are explicitly excluded from the scope of the provisions. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u466	Mr. Viliardos has the floor from the Greek Solution for seven minutes.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u467	Thank you very much, Madam President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, it is clear that creditors through the government want to ensure with today's bill the following: firstly, that the problem of red loans by banks, the public and its institutions is solved through the rapid confiscation of private property without the barrier of justice. Secondly, companies that will take over at degrading prices or which will establish full immunity for their Greek workers - and not only - who will become cheap debt slaves. Thirdly, to completely dismantle the rule of law on the grounds of the decongestion of the courts, recalling that justice has always been blamed by the Troika as a problem that makes Greece unattractive investment. More specifically, with the consultation bill, the government wants to adopt a model that has worked abroad and has now been overshadowed. Generally speaking, it seems to be making legislation in the drawers of ministries or creditors, with the aim of persuading them to produce a project. In particular, the matter of consultation was initiated in the United States with a view to dismantling the courtrooms. At first it was intended to resolve simple business disputes so that they did not end up in the courts. It was, however, slowly extended to all kinds of commercial differences, such as sales of products and services. Finally, as we will analyse below, it had very bad side effects on labour relations. Although consultation has now created several problems on consumer and labour issues, the negative is not the same, but the fact that it can be de facto compulsory. In this regard, even if it is possible for a private person not to sign a service contract, which is subject to mandatory consultation, it may either not know it or not know exactly what it means or take pressure to sign it. For example, the company that wants to work in the recruitment process should make it a prerequisite. In a country with high unemployment, as well as in conditions of exploitation of workers by employers, it is easy to impose compulsory consultation by enterprises as a condition of recruitment. In this case, if the employee refuses to sign a contract of recruitment which presupposes consultation, it is not only the danger of being recruited, but also of being threatened with being placed on the blacklist, so that it is not easy to work in another company that respects that list. Automatically, then, his ability to find a job is being drastically restricted. So he's actually having to sign a contract of this kind. In the example of America, in which experience exists, the main problem created by consultation or the main advantage, whichever is the case, is the confidentiality, which is also provided for in the current bill in Article 5.5. Confidentiality may be desirable from both parties, but it is usually more desirable than from the place that has something to hide. Example: A business that claims in its ads to be the fairest or best to deal with, but in fact it is the opposite. This company adopts the confidential consultation, in which case its potential customers or its employees never learn its specific problems. Even if she loses the extrajudicial dispute, her other potential prosecutors do not learn the arguments that could guarantee them a similar victory, since the consultation minutes are never published. That is precisely why, and not just, the signing of contracts which include consultation in the United States was gradually extended until it finally became compulsory. For example, while only 2% of workers in 1992 signed contracts in consultation, the rate was set at 60% on the American private sector average in 2015. I will submit to the Minutes the first page of a detailed study, which confirms it. [[At this point, the honourable Member, Mr Vassilis Viliardos, presents for the minutes the above document, which is in the archives of the Secretariat Department of the Department of Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] Over the years, many defendants, but also the social group, have found that the consultation has finally evolved into a means of exploiting them, especially by large companies, as regards abusive terms in credit cards, labour contracts, etc. However, society then reacted, so the companies could not maintain the mandatory consultation as much as they wanted, while one of the first companies that resigned from such contracts was GOOGLE in 2018. This has helped to change the legislation with the recent adoption of a provision with the name - in free translation - 'an order to abolish the injustice of compulsory consultation', which is not only about labour issues, but also about consumer, competition and trade conflicts. I'm filing it in the Minutes. [[At this point, the honourable Member, Mr Vassilis Viliardos, presents for the minutes the above document, which is in the archives of the Secretariat Department of the Department of Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] On the basis of the above, the deposit of such a bill in Greece, when it has already been tried abroad and has failed miserably, is the least unacceptable. It is of course understandable that the domestic and international business interests, which the current government serves, want to do so in order to further impoverish the Greeks in the debt colony, the Constitution of which they are constantly trespassing on the memorials they have imposed, but there must be limits somewhere. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] So it is, in fact, a subtle private justice system or the privatisation of justice gradually, especially through the use of ombudsmen, who in the United States are lawyers and not anyone, as the government wants to do with today's bill. For a better understanding of this issue, I would like to point out that ombudsmen usually depend on large businesses, so they usually serve their own interests. I would mention as an example a bank that sues borrowers, which in Greece is only four large banks and thousands of borrowers. Even if it is accidental to choose the ombudsman, if anyone does not like the four banks, it will simply not be chosen. So, he'll do whatever he can to like them, actually no matter what he does for borrowers, if not trap them. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] We had some proposals to make for the bill, but unfortunately my time was over. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u468	Thank you, Mr. Comrade. Mr. Kotrias has the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u469	Many times with high costs and long-term delays the Contracting Parties find that they spend more money even on the amount of their claim. The legal system and environment rather slows down the resolution of disputes and society within the crisis and economic poverty calls for a new alternative to the efficient resolution of disputes. This social requirement has come to cover mediation, a process in which the independent ombudsman helps the parties to achieve a mutually satisfactory solution. It is a civilised, realistic and flexible process, which is already in place in Europe and the United States, where, as the American Arbitration Union says, 85% of cases are being solved. If this process is embraced by the legal and entrepreneurial world, its benefits will be recognised and a modern effective tool for urban differences will undoubtedly be made in the future. Ladies and gentlemen, what I have just read to you is an older greeting, a few years ago, which I made as mayor at a conference on the dissemination of the institution and the procedures of mediation, when it was still an optional institution and a voluntary procedure, but for which the Greek society I think it was never sufficiently informed and perhaps this is an important reason for the fact that until now it does not have the scope we expected. Today, with the resolution of the bill entitled "Measure in civil and commercial matters...", practically answers are given: firstly, all the reasons for the fact that some of the provisions of the previous law were considered unconstitutional, secondly, to the other reasons for not allowing mediation to be established as the prevailing alternative way of resolving disputes, such as in other European countries. Starting in 2008 as a Community directive, 2010 is introduced by law as optional and 2018 is voted as a mandatory institution by Law 4512/2018. Mediation as an alternative way of resolving disputes is obviously not a form of court and offers four things. Firstly, it shortens the time for disputes to be resolved. Let's think that you're on trial in six months, but with mediation you can start right away. Secondly, it dismantles the courts and tribunals and therefore helps to improve the administration of justice and indirectly upgrade the authority of the courts and judges. Thirdly, it costs less, burdening the parties with much less money. Fourthly, it creates a culture of understanding. That is why the first meeting is required at least. If we have to point out something, I would say that the requirement relates to the first meeting, that the compulsory presence of legal persons ensures legal certainty, that the Ombudsman is not a judge, he is a qualified, trained neutral negotiator trying to find a solution to the time and money disputes, that there is always a possibility for the parties to negotiate through courts. Obviously, reservations and remarks have been made during the debate in the Committee on Public Administration. At the hearing I would point out that it was heard that lawyers do not want horizontal application, because they believe that this was the reason why the institution did not go ahead. Nevertheless, the representative of the Piraeus Bar Association said that "we do not believe mediation is a gravel of the law". Secondly, it has been said that there must be a code of conduct for certified ombudsmen and an audit of their education. Thirdly, that it must be supported by the legal world obviously. Fourthly, that it is a voluntary extrajudicial procedure, which has been successfully implemented in the Anglo-Saxon countries and in European countries such as neighbouring Italy, and it relieves the country's burdened judicial system, creating a new culture of settlement of urban differences. To conclude, and despite any reservations that have been made, following the thorough analysis and the debate that has taken place in the committee, I can say today that reservations and questions are being settled and answered, so that in a short time we can vote for a bill that modernises and upgrades our legal system for the benefit of all those involved in the administration of justice, but above all for the benefit of the citizens of our country. Thank you very much.

The parity is typical. The real inequality stems from the unequal economic and social power of each party. Instead of this inequality that exists in practice, as far as possible, to be reduced in the course of appeals to the courts, this inequality is not only maintained but also strengthened, it is essentially brought to an end through the outlaws you are building under the cloak of mediation. Firstly, it follows from the application of this mandatory procedure to all disputes of the one-stop-stop-stop-stop-stop-shopping court in the ordinary course and the majority of family differences with some exceptions. This is why the draft law of the government only seemingly restricts to what is compulsory, whereas in practice it allows for the mediation clause in almost all these disputes. Of course, this agreement will only formally be free because in most cases the weak parties will be forced to accept the mediation clause, for example in a labour contract or an insurance contract. Let's imagine a worker who needs to work. Is there ever a chance that this worker will reject the mediation clause? And is there ever a chance that this worker will not, in the context of mediation, become a victim of pressure and extortion in order to take a solution against him in order to avoid a timely and costly process? Of course not. We are therefore talking about a compulsory private procedure, which is subject to severe financial penalties. Where else does the problem of mediation arise? From the cost of the process. It is 50 euros in its initial phase and 80 euros per annum for the ombudsman, apart from the lawyer's reward, and not even the conclusion of an agreement is certain. We are therefore talking about a great cost that is to be paid to the people in need of recourse to justice. The problem also arises from the very nature of the procedure, since we are talking about a procedure without any guarantee, without any publicity - neither practical - nor any transparency which is not in itself open to dispute settlement proceedings. The principle of publicity is not something that is individual, something marginal, regional. It is a basic procedural principle which is not observed in this case. And where are we driving? It is therefore the procedure that has taken place so that before we vote we can be sure of the completeness of the text and the correctness of legislative intervention. That is precisely the content of the draft law we are discussing today. It is a bill intended to make mediation a credible, alternative method of accessing justice. It is a legal framework that is clear to its objectives and simple to the procedures it incorporates. How could it be different, since the main guidelines it adopts include both the decongestion of the courts and the facilitation of citizens? We are essentially talking about the adoption of a new culture of resolution of urban and commercial disputes, of an economic, confidential and non-discriminatory procedure which does not disrupt, but facilitates family relations, partnership and trade and the relationship of an employer. During this debate in the Committee on Civil Liberties, I mentioned the fact that in the Member States of the European Union where the mediation process is compulsory, there is a reduction in new actions before justice, as well as a significant decongestion of the courts. A reasonable question is therefore whether the introduction of a procedure into Greek legal culture is sufficient. Can an example of good legislation, in other words, prove equally effective in our case? The answer is clear. It can only be provided that it is accompanied by the necessary culture change in the way we deal with the differences between us. It can only be understood that this is a process of friendly and beneficial to individuals. It can only be understood as a conscious choice to resolve disputes and conflicts. It can only be realised that we are able, by means of a structured, concrete and clearly defined procedure, to strike a balance between the parties to the compromise. Finally, even in the event of a failure of the process, the parties will have gained the benefit resulting from their efforts to approach each other. We must not forget, of course, that we are talking about an area which, as a function, process and occupation, is still in an embryonic stage. We are thus given the opportunity, but also the obligation to explain to the citizens the importance of mediation and the many opportunities that it offers us. We give them all that range of information that will enable them to understand the usefulness of this new object and to do it gradually, but also systematically, their estate. Justice is a pillar of democracy and is recognized by Greek citizens as such. However, the difficulties incurred in delaying the adoption of decisions and the long-term duration of a case are pathogens which, at the 2020 threshold, are simply not allowed. With the mediation that we are legislating for today, we are putting an important stone in the effort to tackle these problems. From the field of domestic relations and the window of opportunity to protect children, up to the need to speed up the process of justice, it is up to us to make mediation a distinct choice for citizens. Mediation is the option to facilitate the normalization of interpersonal relations, family, social or professional. It is the solution to the need to harmonize us with European good practice. It is the process that responds to the practical, technical and constitutional issues that the previous legal regime has inherited us. Ladies and gentlemen, the questions that we are answering in this bill are specific. What do we tell the citizens who ask us how to make their everyday lives easier? What alternative are we giving them to organize their lives better? How will our judges be dismantled from their huge volume of work? How can professionals make their work more effective? As a lawyer, a mediator and a member of the National Representation, I cannot ignore the fact that today we are talking about an ambitious intervention, a process capable of improving the day-to-day management of conflicts that social, professional and family life entails, for a new alternative to citizens, but which requires the adoption of a different culture in our legal culture. I am voting for the bill on intermediation of civil and commercial matters for all these reasons and for one more, in order to bring back the feeling that their legislative process is relevant and can only succeed if they embrace it by integrating the logic of mediation into their everyday lives. That must also be our goal from now on. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u499	Madam President, thank you. Ladies and gentlemen, we should be a little more careful and finally stop the mudology that has been started for two, three days, because I think there are people in your party who are the definition of rosyphotological appointment either as members of the previous government or anything else that concerns all of the parties that were this slogan that ruled at that time. [[At this point, the Z' Vice-President of the Parliament, Mr Apostolos ABDELAS, takes over the President's seat.]] Also, let us not forget that even here in the temple of democracy you elected MPs who are eighty years old and over, who would do well not to blame them on the basis of age and not to use them for any reason and to burn them down. As far as the bill is concerned, the mediation institution has been working successfully around the world for decades. It contributes significantly to the rapid out-of-court settlement of civil and commercial matters. The advantages of mediation are many: it enables the parties to balance their interests and find commonly accepted solutions. The procedure shall be carried out within a shorter period of time at a financial cost, escaping the suffering of long-term and long-term judicial struggles, escaping the consequences of the outcome of a trial which may not satisfy either party. And, of course, the more the cases that are resolved out-of-court so much reduce the cases pending in the courts. This also fully responds to the time-consuming demand for speeding up the administration of justice. Simplifying, improving and speeding up the resolution of urban and commercial differences strengthens economic trade, contributes to attracting investment, encourages entrepreneurship, promotes growth as a whole. Ladies and gentlemen, since I started working on my common priorities, it is to contribute to progress and development. The region that I have the honour to represent, Piraeus and the islands, needs a dynamic investment programme to create many stable and well-paid jobs. However, throughout the past period, whenever I spoke with entrepreneurs to promote new projects and programmes, there were often reservations and concerns. One of the most serious reasons for the failure to develop new business was delays in the administration of justice. By applying mediation in practice, the benefits of enterprises will be multiple. Intermediation is the most appropriate institution for normalising situations, where the parties involved may have a different perception, without anyone's fairness or wrongdoing, and most of all when there is expectation and future cooperation, so it is in the interests of both parties to avoid disputes. The draft law we are voting on today is necessary for too many reasons. With the initial incorporation of the European Mediation Directive in 2010, the legislator, hesitant in taking effective measures, has made it difficult to move it forward. That is why, after more than eight years, there has been no culture of out-of-court settlement of cases, hence the number of mediation that remains too low. Since 2018, the legislator has been reinstated and provided for the phase of compulsory submission of certain disputes in the mediation process. However, this law has never been implemented in practice because, on the one hand, its power has been suspended and, on the other hand, its provisions on compulsoryity, as they were stated, were found to be unconstitutional by the administrative plenary session of the Ice Bay. And the Court of Justice of the European Union recently came to judge several of the limitations of the relevant legislation as being contrary to European law. Ladies and gentlemen, all these issues must be dealt with in order to resolve the practical and technical issues of the previous laws, in order to obtain a modern legal framework in accordance with the decisions of the Ice Bay and the Court of Justice of the European Union and - most importantly - to strengthen citizens' confidence in the institution. Thus, the proposed draft law puts into effect a new integrated system for the application of mediation. We facilitate citizens' access to it, subject to certain categories of cases at a compulsory initial mediation session with specific guarantees, but also with clear penalties in case of circumvention of the procedure provided for. We ensure that if mediation does not result in a mutually acceptable solution, the parties will fully maintain the right to appeal to justice. We are setting the appropriate framework for ensuring a high level of training of ombudsmen. Already the administrative plenary of the Ice Bay unanimously delivered an opinion that the draft law is in line with the Constitution. This development, which even comes before the vote on the present, is very important, because we in the New Democracy above all believe in legitimacy. It is not enough that what we are doing is actually only right from a point of view. Everything we do must be in line with the Constitution. We must also respect the country's international obligations. This is how Greece will become a modern state. Institutions will thus work effectively without backwards, without the need for constant changes in legislation and post-respect for judicial decisions. Ladies and gentlemen, it does not mean speeding up the administration of justice without dismantling the courts. And it does not mean decongestion of the courts, without dissemination and application of alternative forms of dispute settlement. For all these reasons, we must support the mediation institution. Today in Parliament, by voting on the bill, and tomorrow in society with the encouragement of citizens and businesses to start implementing it in practice. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-28-S1-commons.u560	Ladies and gentlemen, I think that we have actually completed a thorough discussion process of a bill which, at least for the majority of colleagues, but also for the parties of the Greek Parliament, is a need for today's reality in our homeland. I am not saying this because, as I have repeated many times, there has been a similar legislative initiative from a previous government, from the 2010 PASOK or SYRIZA of 2018, but mainly because there is no serious country in Europe and the world that has not adopted the institution of mediation as a key process of credible alternative and additional method of resolving disputes, without access to justice. This is an assumption that I think no one can question. I understand the logic of 'anything that is European I do not adopt.' I am obviously talking about the position of the Communist Party of Greece. I disagree. I respect this view, and I imagine that all of what is heard as objections from there and beyond stop on this basic assumption. However, let us actually see what has evolved these days in the relevant parliamentary committee so far in plenary with regard to this bill. The parties that have ruled in a previous period and have attempted this legislative initiative and want to adopt in general a reasonable philosophy regarding the bill. Is that legitimate, but how legitimate it is to say that we copy a previous bill when ten bodies come during the hearing and congratulate us on a new, self-evident, comprehensive bill? On the other hand, how much is it that you do not take into account matters which in fact relate to either European legislation or constitutionality internally? I say this, ladies and gentlemen, because you have raised to me issues relating to the properties of the ombudsmen, to issues relating to training providers, which you know have responded at European level. Do you not know that in Europe ombudsmen do not even need to have a higher education degree? You don't know that? Do you not know that, when there was the first PASOK law, we were actually judged on issues of this kind at European level and indeed on the different viewpoint of the European institutions? Don't you know all this? Don't you know that we had to adapt to a bill on data that exists throughout Europe? I say this because I repeat that I accept the criticism of the Opposition no matter what it is in any way, but not at least on matters that are far from reality. Ladies and gentlemen, the aim is to change the legal culture in our country and I urge you to take responsibility for all. It is not just a question of bringing in a new institution that will obviously help, create a different logic and will certainly unload the enormous burden that exists in Greek justice. Obviously, this is a big issue. The question is to be able to promote a different culture, which will enable us to introduce a different spirit into Greek society, but also to the officials of justice, that of friendly and out-of-court dispute resolution. This is the philosophy of this particular bill, which we are trying today essentially to vote in the Greek Parliament, so that the next day we can see all the good things we describe in each of our own ways will have a substantial effect on Greek reality. The effort made by the Ministry of Justice was not to copy a previous bill. It was to present an autonomous, comprehensive draft law, which describes all the terms and rules of the operation of this institution. I think it is really a great success for the Greek parliamentary reality that at least most parties have stood up to this logic, despite criticism and the fact that different opinions have been expressed. However, I repeat that we must also seriously and responsibly see the effort being made, because only then can we be meaningful and useful to Greek society. There have been many issues that one might have raised and which one could have commented on and discussed, but I think most of the opposition of the arguments has been made by both the rapporteurs and the parliamentary representatives. It is important, however, to see what has happened in the neighbouring country in Italy. In 2010, which took the respective legislative initiative in Greece, it was precisely then that mediation was adopted as an institution in Italy. It spent more than two years with terrible internal reactions and many problems, which showed that it could be very difficult to implement the institution. I imagine you realize where the reactions usually stand when we try to introduce something new and when this new may affect some. Today, ladies and gentlemen, in Italy more than two hundred and fifty thousand cases per year are resolved with the mediation institution. Do you realize how much weight is deducted from the regular justice procedure? That is what we need to stand by, and that is where we need to demarcate whatever objective we are currently putting on this legislative initiative. I say this because I say again that the opposition is good, I accept it, it is good the different opinion and the different expression of opinion, but I think that reality is always one, it is indisputable and no one can escape from it. The sooner and easier we see it, we recognize it, we adopt it and we support it, the greater the chances are that we can help the Greek society through these legislative initiatives. I will say one more thing about the bill: we are not talking about compulsory mediation. I've said it many times, and I'm glad you understand that, Mr. Blondopoulos.
